Let me think about the proof strategy.

The language: locally nameless STLC with Bool, arrows, if-then-else. CBV semantics.

Note the tricky part: `value` requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` and step rules have `locally_closed t2` premises etc.

Key challenge: In locally nameless setting, `open t v` substitution. The logical relation approach: define a unary logical relation on terms indexed by type, using `SN` and `value`, standard Girard/Tait style for CBV:

- `LR Bool t := SN t /\ (value t -> ...)`? Actually for CBV, a common definition: 
  - `LR Bool t := SN t` (since closed booleans, SN implies... hmm, we want to show that if empty |-- t \in Bool then SN t). Actually for the fundamental theorem we need: for well-typed term t of type T with closing substitution satisfying LR at contexts, t in LR T. Then LR T ⊆ SN.

For CBV, standard: LR at base type = SN; LR at arrow A→B = SN t /\ (value t -> forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t u)) — actually more precisely:

`LR (A→B) t := SN t /\ forall u, value u -> LR A u -> LR B (t u)`.

Hmm, but actually since SN t implies... For the fundamental theorem at T_Abs, we need: lambda is a value, and when applied to v with LR A v, we need LR B (open t v). `open t v` is not `tm_app (abs) v` but reduces to it in one step. Standard approach: define LR on closed terms via the interpretation where `LR (A→B) t := SN t /\ (forall u, value u -> LR A u -> LR B (tm_app t u))`. Then for a value abstraction v = abs T t, we need LR B (open t u) — from `tm_app (abs T t) u --> open t u`, and SN of app implies SN of open t u (since SN closed under forward step). And for the "forall u" part, we need to show app of value and u is in LR B, which we get from the fundamental lemma applied to the body under substitution.

But this requires substitution lemma machinery for locally nameless, which is quite heavy. Alternative: define LR directly on open terms? There's another approach: use `tm_app v u` rather than `open t u` in the LR definition for arrows, and prove:

- LR T ⊆ SN (for closed terms, actually all terms).
- If LR T t then SN t.
- If t -->* t' then LR T t -> LR T t'? (backward closure: SN t' -> SN t.)
- Fundamental: for any Gamma, t, T with Gamma |-- t \in T, for any substitution env sigma with forall x, Gamma x = Some S -> LR S (sigma x) and all sigma x closed & values etc. — but locally nameless typing uses open with fvar, so we need the "fvar-based" interpretation:

Standard locally nameless: the fundamental theorem is proved for `Gamma |-- t \in T` where the environment maps fvars to terms in LR. Since T_Abs introduces `x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2` for all fresh x, we instantiate x with a fresh fvar and then need the induction hypothesis with environment mapping x to a variable (fvar x) that is in LR T1 — provided fvar x is in LR T1. We need `LR T (tm_fvar x)` for fresh x. Hmm, that requires that fvars are in LR. But LR at arrow requires SN (fvar) which is trivially SN since fvar doesn't step (no rule reduces a bare fvar). Yes! SN (tm_fvar x) holds (no steps). And value (tm_fvar x) is false, so the arrow condition vacuous. For Bool, LR Bool = SN, so fvar is in LR Bool too. So fvars of any type are in LR? For arrow: need SN fvar — yes — and forall u value, LR A u -> LR B (app (fvar x) u). But app (fvar x) u does not reduce... Hmm, is app (fvar x) u in LR B? We need it to be. That's not SN necessarily? For SN: app (fvar x) u is SN iff u is SN (no step applies at the app since fvar isn't a value and doesn't step; ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1', impossible). Actually app (fvar x) u is stuck: it's SN iff... wait SN of a stuck term whose subterm u isn't reachable by steps: since no rule applies, app (fvar x) u has no reductions, so it's SN trivially via SN_intro with no successors! Right: SN is defined on reduction, so any normal form is SN regardless of subterms. So app (fvar x) u is SN (vacuously, no steps). And LR B (app (fvar x) u) requires SN (trivially yes) plus arrow-condition if B is arrow: for all v value, LR B2 (app (app (fvar x) u) v) — again stuck, SN. Hmm, so structurally, for fvar-headed applications, everything is vacuously SN and LR at any type. But we need to prove this by induction on type: "stuck terms with fvar head are in LR". Actually simpler: prove `LR T (tm_fvar x)` and if `LR T u`... hmm, we need a general lemma: any term t with no possible step is in LR T? That's false: LR at arrow also requires the applied form to be in LR. So if t doesn't step and isn't a value, app t u doesn't step. By induction on type, "if t is SN and (not value or ...)" hmm.

Rather than the "LR on fvars" approach, an alternative cleaner approach for locally nameless: define the logical relation over closed terms via a "closing substitution"/semantic environment, and handle opening with fvars by interpreting fvars via an environment. That is: define

`env_LR (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x)`.

Then fundamental theorem: Gamma |-- t \in T -> forall sigma, env_LR Gamma sigma -> ... but `t` has fvars; we need to close t under sigma: subst sigma t. So we need a substitution function and its interaction with open, which is the standard locally nameless "subst" machinery: subst of fvar, opening lemma: subst sigma (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst (update sigma x u) t) u, requires t is locally closed? For locally nameless, this requires that the body doesn't have dangling bvars — T_Abs's body is arbitrary! Hmm, in the supplied typing rules, T_Abs doesn't require t1 locally closed. So open t1 (fvar x) may have dangling bvars. Hmm, but for the fundamental theorem, we generally first prove a "well-typed terms are locally closed" lemma! Actually is that true here? T_Abs rule: `(forall x, ~ In x L -> x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2)`. The body t1 itself need not be locally closed? Hmm, if t1 = bvar 0, then open t1 (fvar x) = fvar x, well typed. And tm_abs T1 t1 is not locally closed. But value (tm_abs T t) requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t), i.e., lc_at 1 t. So a well-typed lambda whose body has dangling bvars would not be a value! Interesting — the semantics `ST_AppAbs` requires locally_closed of the abstraction. So such a term app would be stuck (not SN issue though — stuck is SN).

Hmm, so can we prove well-typed terms are locally closed? Let's think: T_Var: fvar is locally closed. T_Abs: need lc_at 1 t1 from the premise that for all fresh x, lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)). Hmm, does that follow? If t1 = bvar 1, then open t1 (fvar x) = bvar 1 (since k=0, i=1 ≠ 0, so it stays), which is not locally closed, so cannot be typed. In general, lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)) implies lc_at 1 t1 — this is a standard "close" property: if open t1 with a fresh fvar is locally closed then t1 is lc_at 1. Provable by induction on t1. Good.

So well-typed terms are locally closed. Then values: abs of well-typed... Well, need values in LR.

Actually the lemma holds when... Let's think: In locally nameless, `open` replaces bvar 0 with u. Substituting fvars with sigma, and then opening with u. On the right: subst (sigma,x ↦ u) t replaces fvar x (the free fvar named x in t) with u, others with sigma; then open replaces bvar 0 with u. Hmm, these are different substitutions! The real standard substitution lemma in LN: `open (subst (sigma, x := u) t) u = subst (sigma) (open t (fvar x))`? Standard route: prove LR is compatible with substitution of *values* into *locally closed* terms. Alternative clean approach: skip general substitution and instead prove SN directly via a "strong normalization by hereditary" approach working with terms containing fvars and proving closure under substitution semantically. I think the LN "open" approach is standard: In Software Foundations' LN chapter, they define a relation on *terms with fvars* via "body" etc. The typical fundamental theorem for LN STLC in SF uses `lc_at` and a relation `V` where `V T := SN` etc. Hmm.

Actually, let me consider another angle: could we avoid LR entirely and prove SN by induction on typing with a "all terms of type T with free vars substituted by SN values are SN" formulation? The standard Tait-style proof needs a semantic relation, since syntactic SN is not preserved by application directly. Let me plan the LR carefully.

Since terms may have both fvars and bvars (bvars only under binders; arbitrary in general, but well-typed terms are locally closed and opening yields lc terms), let's define:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t   (* or SN t /\ (neutral stuff)? *)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      SN t /\ (forall u, value u -> LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Hmm, for Bool: for the fundamental theorem at T_If: t1 : Bool, t2, t3 : T. IH: LR Bool t1 (i.e. SN t1), LR T t2, LR T t3. Need LR T (if t1 then t2 else t3). With LR Bool = SN, we'd need to show: if SN t1 and LR T t2, LR T t3, then LR T (if t1 then t2 else t3). For T = Bool, that's SN (if ...) which requires: if SN t1, SN t2, SN t3 then SN (if t1 t2 t3). That's plausible: any step goes to t1' with SN t1' (from SN t1), or (if t1 is true/false...) hmm. Need an induction on SN t1. For T arrow, we additionally need: forall u value, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app (if ...) u). But the if term with an arrow type: `if t1 then t2 else t3` applied to u steps? Only ST_App1 if the if-term steps; if t1 is a normal form (not a value, since true/false are values? if t1 is true, the if steps). Hmm: the app of an if-term can step via ST_App1 when the if steps. If the if-term is a stuck normal form (e.g., t1 = fvar x? no, fvars don't occur in well-typed closed... but at type Bool they could in intermediate terms). Hmm, to show LR at arrow, we need SN (app t u) where t = if...; steps of app t u: either t steps (ST_App1) or t is value — if-term is never a value — or... So SN(app t u) follows from SN(t) essentially. And the inner arrow condition: app (if...) u is itself a candidate for LR T2; for the arrow-condition at LR T2 if T2 is an arrow, we'd need LR T3 (app (app (if...) u) v) — this again follows if we know a general "SN-head" lemma.

I think the cleanest is to prove a general lemma: "neutral-ish" terms are in LR at every type if they are SN and their applications... Hmm, this can get complicated. Let's simplify by defining LR at arrow with just SN plus a "step-closed/expansion" property, and proving general lemmas:

Key insight: For CBV SN, the standard approach for a *call-by-value* language uses LR with the value condition. Let me define:

```
LR Bool t := SN t
LR (A → B) t := SN t /\ forall u, value u -> LR A u -> LR B (tm_app t u)
```

We need these lemmas:
(L1) LR T t -> SN t. By induction on T.
(L2) LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'. Backward/forward closure under steps: from SN t we get SN t' and for arrow, for each u: LR B (app t u) → need LR B (app t' u), which follows from app t u --> app t' u whenever locally_closed u. Hmm! ST_App1 requires locally_closed u. Uh oh. We need locally_closed of the argument. But at this level, u is in LR A and value. Value u means u is abs (locally closed), true, or false — all locally closed! value → locally_closed. Good, we can prove `value t -> locally_closed t`. Then app t u --> app t' u holds by ST_App1 with the lc premise. 

Hmm wait, actually we don't need L2 with the step premise for app directly; we need: SN t and "steps preserve LR". Let's structure it:

(L_sn_step) SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'. Trivial by inversion on SN.

(LR_step) LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'. Induction on T; Bool trivial; arrow: SN t → SN t'; forall u value, LR A u → LR B (app t' u): we have LR B (app t u), and app t u --> app t' u using ST_App1 (requires locally_closed u — from value u). Then apply IH (on T2 = B) to get LR B (app t' u). Wait, IH is on the type B, i.e., the statement "forall t t', LR B t -> t --> t' -> LR B t'". Yes, induction on T where the arrow case uses IH for the codomain B. Fine — induction on ty structure.

(LR_anti_step / expansion) LR T t' -> t --> t' -> LR T t. i.e., LR closed under one-step backward expansion. This is the crucial "beta-expansion" property. For Bool: SN t' → SN t (since t steps to t', SN intro). For arrow: need SN t and forall u value LR A u → LR B (app t u). Given LR B (app t' u) and app t u --> app t' u → use IH on B. And SN t from SN t' — need forall t'', t --> t'' -> SN t'': if t'' = t' (the step) then SN t'; other steps t --> t''? The term t might have multiple reducts; we only know one step t --> t'. So SN t requires all reducts SN. Hmm! For a general t, one step to t' doesn't imply all reducts are SN... wait, SN t is defined as all t'' with t --> t'' are SN. If t --> t' and t' is SN, is t SN? No! t might also step to a non-SN term. E.g., t = if true then Ω else true? CBV: if true then Ω else true --> Ω (not SN) and also? no other steps. Hmm, need an example with two reducts: in CBV deterministic? The step relation here: if-true steps to t1; if-term steps under ST_If only if t1 steps. Hmm, it's deterministic-ish. But there's ST_App1 and ST_App2 which are not overlapping? ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1'; ST_App2 requires value t1. Can a term be both? t1 --> t1' and value t1 simultaneously — no, values don't step (do they?). Values: abs (lc), true, false — none step. So determinism holds? App abs v: ST_AppAbs applies, also ST_App2? ST_App2 requires v1 value; app (abs..) v could step by ST_App2 if v --> v', but v is a value so no. ST_App1 requires abs --> t1', impossible since abs is value and values don't step. So app (abs) v steps only via ST_AppAbs. So step is deterministic if values don't step and every term is either a value or ... Hmm, but do we need determinism? "Backward expansion" would follow from determinism + SN t'. But is it truly needed in general form? The standard approach for SN proofs: you need "if t --> t' and LR t' then LR t" only in the specific beta-expansion form (app (abs t) v --> open t v) and congruence cases, or you prove it using the SN induction.

Standard Tait proof: fundamental theorem at T_Abs requires showing LR (A→B) (λx.t). The arrow LR requires SN(λx.t) and forall v value in LR A, LR B (app (λx.t) v). We have from IH: LR B (open t (fvar x)) for fresh x. Then use the "substitution/open" lemma: LR B (open t v). And LR B (app (λx.t) v) follows from the beta-expansion lemma applied with the step app (λx.t) v --> open t v. So we need expansion only for terms that are specifically app of an abstraction applied to a value... but if we state it as "LR T t' -> t --> t' -> LR T t" we'd need determinism.

Alternatively, avoid expansion: prove `LR B (tm_app (tm_abs T t) v)` directly by using the reflexive-transitive closure. Hmm.

Actually, SN is "closed under reverse steps" only with determinism. But here's a cleaner route: define LR at arrow to include SN t and the application property, and prove the beta case via a lemma:

`LR B (open t v) -> LR B (tm_app (tm_abs T t) v)` requires SN of app: since app steps to open t v (and possibly other steps?). Steps of app (abs) v: ST_AppAbs (needs value v and lc abs), ST_App1 impossible (abs is value → doesn't step), ST_App2 needs v --> v', impossible since v value → doesn't step. So app (abs) v has exactly one reduct, open t v. So SN follows. The arrow-condition for app (abs) v: forall w value LR A' w, LR B' (app (app (abs) v) w). This outer app steps to open t v applied to w... recursively. Hmm, this nested condition is annoying. But note: LR B (open t v) already includes the arrow conditions for open t v (if B is arrow), and step-preservation gives them for app (abs t) v since app (abs t) v --> open t v in exactly one step... and we need LR B' (app (app (abs t) v) w) from LR B (app (open t v) w)? app (app (abs t) v) w --> app (open t v) w via ST_App1 with lc w (value w → lc). So if we have "LR closed under forward step", we get from LR B (app (open t v) w) that LR B (app (app (abs t) v) w)... no wait, forward step: LR t → t --> t' → LR t'. We want LR of the source (app (app (abs t) v) w) given LR of the target (app (open t v) w). That's backward again, but at the level of app chains: is the source's reduct set exactly the target? For app x w where x --> x' and w is a value: steps are ST_App1 (x --> x') and ... ST_App2 needs value x — no. ST_AppAbs needs x = abs — no. So the only reducts of app x w are app x'' w for x --> x''. Thus SN(app x w) iff (forall x'', x --> x'' -> SN (app x'' w)). Since x = app (abs t) v has only one reduct open t v, app x w has exactly one reduct: app (open t v) w. So SN transfers backward here.

This suggests a general lemma: "deterministic step" → SN t' → t --> t' → (all reducts of t are SN) → SN t. Hmm.

Maybe there's a much simpler approach: since step is deterministic (is it?), prove determinism and then use "SN is closed under expansion". Let's check determinism more carefully. Two rules could apply to the same term in different ways?
- `tm_app (tm_abs T t) v`: ST_AppAbs (if value v and lc abs) and ST_App1 needs tm_abs --> something: values don't step, so no. ST_App2 needs value (tm_abs T t) AND v --> v'. If v is a value (needed for AppAbs) then v doesn't step. But if v is not a value, AppAbs doesn't apply; then App1 needs abs --> ..., no; App2 needs value abs and v --> v' — yes applies. So unique.
- `tm_app t1 t2` where t1 is not abs-valued: App1 (t1 --> t1') vs App2 (value t1 and t2 --> t2'). If value t1 and t1 --> t1': impossible (values don't step). OK.
But wait, App1's conclusion in this system is app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2; that requires t1 --> t1'. Unique by IH. App2's conclusion app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2', unique by IH. But could App1 and App2 both derive the same term? Only if value t1 and t1 steps, no.
- if: IfTrue (t1 = true), IfFalse (t1 = false), ST_If (t1 --> t1'). true/false don't step, and true ≠ false. Unique.
So step is deterministic — provided "values don't step" (provable: values: abs with lc, true, false; abs --> requires abs be app/if; none of the step conclusions is an abs? ST_AppAbs concludes app; ST_App1/2 conclude app; ST_IfTrue/False conclude t1/t2 which could be anything... wait, tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1, so a value true steps? OK so let's step back and design properly.

Standard CBV LR (for strongly normalizing STLC, e.g., in Harper's PFPL or Charguéraud's "Normalization by evaluation" or the LN chapter in SF), the "semantic type" is:

```
V(A) = set of values of type A (closed)
V(Bool) = {true, false}
V(A→B) = { λx.t | forall v ∈ V(A), t[v/x] ∈ E(B) }
E(A) = { t | t -->* v, v ∈ V(A) }  (closed terms)
```

and SN ⊇ E(A). For a SN proof we can define:
- `LR T t := SN t /\ (value t -> VL T t)`? Hmm.

Let me define instead "hereditarily" on values:

```
V T : tm -> Prop   (* values in the semantic type *)
V Bool v := SN v  (all values SN anyway; or just value for Bool?)
V (A→B) v := value v /\ SN v /\ forall u, V A u -> LR B (app v u)
```
Hmm, with LN and open terms, "value u" is needed for substitutions.

with the "value u" premise restored (so that app v u reduces in one step to the body). Then T_App needs app_LR with non-value t2 — using induction on SN t2 (and SN t1). Let's examine feasibility:

`app_LR : LR (Arrow A B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2)`.

Proof: Let's induct on SN t1 and SN t2. Hmm, two nested inductions. Let's think about what's needed:
- If t1 is a value and t2 is a value: VR gives it ✓.
- If t1 is a value, t2 not a value: app t1 t2: reducts via App2 (t2 --> t2'). Need LR B (app t1 t2') by IH on SN t2, with LR A t2' (forward closure) ✓.
- If t1 not a value, t2 value: reducts via App1 (t1 --> t1') — need LR B (app t1' t2) by IH on SN t1 ✓. Also AppAbs impossible since t1 not value (abs+lc would be value). ✓
- Both not values: reducts via App1 only (App2 needs value t1) ✓; IH on SN t1.
So induction on SN t1 with the statement generalized over t2, but the case "t1 value, t2 not value" needs induction on SN t2, which is fine if we do the inner induction on SN t2 with the hypothesis LR (Arrow A B) t1 and value t1 → VR... Good, both lemmas need `value/¬value` decidability (for the case split), and forward closure, and "values don't step".

Hmm, thinking about it more, maybe there's a way to make the if case easy by proving a general "expansion" lemma using determinism:

**Key lemma via determinism**: `SN t' -> t --> t' -> (forall t'', t --> t'' -> t'' = t') -> SN t`. Or more usefully:

`SN_anti : (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') <-> SN t` trivially. And with determinism, to prove SN t it suffices to find the unique reduct.

**General plan using determinism**: Prove `step_det : t --> t1 -> t --> t2 -> t1 = t2`. Then SN is closed under backward steps. Also, a term is SN iff its unique reduct (if any) is SN. This makes showing SN of a term easy: identify its reduct(s). But LR still needs the type structure.

**Design v2**: Use a *step-indexed-free* but type-directed LR with these clauses:

LR Bool t := SN t

- LR ⊆ SN ✓ (part of def).

General lemma: `forall (body : tm) (x ...), ...` hmm, let's state:

`LR_app_abs : forall A B body T1, (forall u, value u -> LR A u -> LR B (open body u)) -> forall u, LR A u -> LR B (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u)`.

Proof: induction on SN u (using LR_sn). Cases:
- value u: app has unique reduct open body u; LR B (open body u) given ✓; then LR B (app ...) by anti-reduction (since app --> open body u with lc abs? The anti-reduction lemma's step premise is `app (abs T1 body) u --> open body u` which requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body)` and `value u` — both available [need lc body from typing]). ✓
- not value u: need LR B (app abs u) = SN ∧ arrow clause. SN: SN_intro; for t' with app --> t': inversion gives App2 case (u --> u', t' = app abs u'), AppAbs (needs value u ✗), App1 (needs abs step ✗). So t' = app abs u'; by IH on SN u' (from SN u) and LR A u' (forward closure) ✓. Arrow clause: forall w ∈ LR B, LR C (app (app abs u) w): hmm, we need this now. app (app abs u) w with u not a value: hmm, this requires more work. Since app abs u is SN but not a value, app (app abs u) w... 

  Hmm, maybe better to prove the arrow clause generically: if LR B (app abs u) is needed with B = Arrow B1 B2, then we need forall w ∈ LR B1, LR B2 (app (app abs u) w). Note app (app abs u) w reduces only when app abs u reduces (App1) [since app abs u is not a value] or when app abs u is a value (no) — wait App2 requires value (app abs u) ✗. So the only reducts are app (app abs u') w when app abs u --> app abs u', i.e., when u --> u'. Hmm, but App1's premise requires locally_closed w — need LR → lc.

  So we can do a nested SN induction on u for the whole statement `forall w ∈ LR B1, LR B2 (app (app abs u) w)`. Ugh, this is getting deep. It's provable but messy.

This suggests that the "forall u ∈ LR A" (no value premise) formulation requires lots of nested inductions. The "value premise" formulation has the T_If problem. Hmm.

**Let me think about a cleaner, well-known technique for CBV SN: the "Girard-style" with a single LR clause that includes both value and non-value behavior via a "neutral" notion.**

Standard technique: define
```
LR T t := SN t /\ (value t -> VR T t)
```
where VR for arrows is: `forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app v u)`. Wait, we discussed a gap with values that arise as reducts. Let's re-examine: the gap was: LR (Arrow A B) t' where t' is a value and t --> t' with t ∈ LR (Arrow A B) t. To prove VR t', we need forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t' u). Hmm.

What if VR is defined as: `forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app v u)` and LR Bool t := SN t? Then consider t = if true then abs1 else abs2 ∈ LR (Arrow A B) — wait, is it? LR arrow = SN ∧ (value → VR); the if is not a value, so LR (Arrow A B) (if ...) = SN (if ...) — meaning at arrow type, ANY SN term is in LR (Arrow A B)?? That's wrong! Because then the T_App rule would let us apply a stuck if-term... hmm, but for SN purposes that's fine? If a term at arrow type is SN, and we apply it to an argument in LR, is the app SN? If t is SN and u is SN, is app t u SN? Not necessarily: t = abs (λx. Ω) is SN (abs never steps) and u SN, but app abs u --> Ω not SN. That's why the arrow clause needs the beta requirement. But with the value-condition formulation, LR (Arrow A B) (abs) requires VR of the abs which includes the beta requirement ✓, whereas the if-term at arrow type only requires SN, and the app of the if-term... T_App with t1 = if-term (not a value): app (if ...) u: only App1 steps — so SN (app) follows from SN (if) and... app (if...) u is SN iff for all reducts if' of the if, SN (app if' u). Is that guaranteed by SN (if...)? We need: for every reduct chain, applying to u keeps SN — not automatic! E.g., if true then abs_Ω else true: SN (if ...) requires SN abs_Ω ✓ (abs never steps). So if-term ∈ LR (Arrow A B) under the value-formulation. Then app (if term) u --> app abs_Ω u --> Ω ✗. So SN (app) fails. But do we ever need LR (Arrow) (if-term)? In T_App, if t1 is an if-term typed at arrow: LR (Arrow T1 T2) t1 (from IH) = SN only. Then T_App demands LR T2 (app t1 t2) — which would be false in this example. So the value-formulation is WRONG: LR arrow must constrain non-value terms too.

Indeed: the value-condition must be: `t ∈ LR(A→B) iff t ∈ SN and ∀u ∈ LR A, app t u ∈ LR B` — for ALL t, not just values. That's formulation 2. And then for a value abstraction v = λx.body, the requirement is `∀u ∈ LR A, app v u ∈ LR B`, proved via cases on u (value or not) as I started. So formulation 2 is the correct one. The cost: nested inductions for app of non-value arguments.

Hmm OK. Let's think about the structure of these nested inductions more carefully, and find the cleanest general lemmas.

Actually, here's a classic trick to handle this: prove a general lemma

**Lemma (LR_app)**: `LR (A→B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u)`.

If we define LR arrow to include this directly (as formulation 2 does), this lemma is *immediate by definition* — no induction needed. Then all the T_App case is trivial. The hard work moves to T_Abs (proving the abstraction ∈ LR arrow), which needs: forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app (λ.body) u). And LR B (app λ.body u) = SN + arrow clauses. The key sub-lemma needed: for a value u ∈ LR A, LR B (open body u) is given (from IH), and we need to "back-propagate" from open body u to app (λ.body) u, and this must work *at all types B*, including arrows where further app conditions arise.

So define the general "beta-expansion" lemma:

**Lemma (beta-expansion)**: `forall B (v : tm) (u : tm) (body : tm)`, if `v = tm_abs T body`? and `locally_closed v` and `value u`? and `LR B (open body u)`, then `LR B (app v u)`.

Proof by induction on the type B:
- B = Bool: SN (app v u) from SN (open body u): app's unique reduct is open body u (needs lc v and value u) ✓.
- B = Arrow B1 B2: need SN (app v u) ✓ same; and forall w ∈ LR B1, LR B2 (app (app v u) w). Given w ∈ LR B1: hmm. Use IH with B := B2? We have LR (Arrow B1 B2) (open body u) → its arrow clause gives LR B2 (app (open body u) w) ✓. And we need LR B2 (app (app v u) w). Note app (app v u) w --> app (open body u) w via App1 (requires lc w — need LR → lc!). Hmm, or instead: app (app v u) w --? Is there a direct beta? No. So we need anti-reduction at type B2. Alternatively apply the IH for B2 with... hmm, we need something of the form "LR B2 t' -> t --> t' -> LR B2 t" (anti-reduction). 

So anti-reduction (backward closure under a step) is needed anyway. Let's see: anti-reduction at type T: LR T t' -> t --> t' -> LR T t. Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: SN t' → SN t (needs determinism / all reducts of t are SN). Hmm, t --> t' but t may have other reducts; with determinism only t'. So need determinism. Alternatively, if we know t --> t' and SN t', to show SN t we need all reducts SN; determinism gives it.
  Hmm, but maybe we can avoid determinism by strengthening the hypothesis: `(forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t` (i.e., LR is closed under "all reducts in LR"). This is the natural closure property! Indeed SN_intro is exactly: (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t. And for arrow: if all reducts of t are in LR (Arrow A B), show LR: SN ✓ (from all reducts SN); forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u): need SN and arrow-clauses; app t u's reducts: if t steps to t' → app t' u ∈ LR B (from hypothesis + arrow clause at t'); if t is abs and u a value → open body u; if t value and u steps → ... So we need a similar "all reducts" analysis. This is essentially a generalized anti-reduction: if all one-step reducts of t are in LR T then t ∈ LR T. This property, call it `LR_snoc`, can be proven by induction on T? Let's see for arrow: assume H : forall t', t --> t' -> LR (Arrow A B) t'. Goal LR (Arrow A B) t = SN t ∧ forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u).
  - SN t: SN_intro; apply H to get LR → SN ✓.
  - forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u): prove by SN_intro (well-founded induction on SN u? or on what?). Reducts of app t u: 
    (a) t --> t': app t' u, and LR (Arrow A B) t' gives the arrow clause: LR B (app t' u) ✓.
    (b) value t, u --> u': app t u', IH on SN u' with LR A u' ✓.
    (c) t = abs T' body with lc, u value: hmm, this is the beta case: the reduct is open body u, and we need LR B (open body u). But do we know anything about open body u? We know LR (Arrow A B) (app t u) — no wait, that's what we're proving. Hmm. From H we know LR (Arrow A B) t = SN t ∧ forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u) — the arrow clause at t gives LR B (app t u) — circular! Yes: in the arrow case with all-reducts hypothesis, proving LR B (app t u) requires... hmm. Actually wait. Let's redo: we want LR B (app t u) where t ∈ ... we're trying to prove LR (Arrow A B) t. Given u ∈ LR A. Reducts of app t u: case (c) requires LR B (open body u). We know from H: for t' = ... hmm, t doesn't necessarily step. H gives LR (Arrow A B) t' for reducts t' of t, not about t itself.

  So the "all reducts" property doesn't suffice at arrow because we can't get the beta-reduct info. Unless... in case (c), t = abs T' body: we need to know something about body. H can't give it since abs doesn't step.

  Hmm, this shows: any LR at arrow must *positively* constrain the bodies of abstractions. So when t ∈ LR (Arrow A B) is an abstraction, we must have the body info. With H only constraining reducts, the abs is unconstrained. But is an abs reachable as a reduct of a non-value term? Yes (if true then abs else ...).

  Hmm! So how do we ever get LR (Arrow A B) (abs ...) after a step, e.g., in Lemma A/B recursive steps? We need t' ∈ LR at arrow. If t --> t' with t' = abs, we get LR (Arrow A B) t' only if the LR definition constrains the abs. So anti-reduction at arrow types needs VR of values, which is the "positive" info. But that info can't be derived from the all-reducts hypothesis (since abs has no reducts). Hmm. So anti-reduction LR T t' -> t --> t' -> LR T t at arrow type where t' = abs requires: LR (Arrow A B) t' from the hypothesis (positive), and then LR (Arrow A B) t needs its own arrow clause which follows from LR (Arrow A B) t' via forward steps (for reducts) — wait: LR (Arrow A B) t needs forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t u). Hmm, but here t --> t' so app t u --> app t' u (App1, needs lc u). So anti-reduction at B applied to the app terms. And by induction on the type B this is fine (B is smaller than Arrow A B). And the base of the induction = Bool: SN t' → SN t needs determinism (all reducts of t are SN). Hmm, OR we can prove SN t from "t --> t' and SN t'" using determinism.

So anti-reduction needs determinism (for the SN part), and positive VR for values... wait no: anti-reduction at arrow: `LR (Arrow A B) t' -> t --> t' -> LR (Arrow A B) t`:
  - SN t from SN t' (determinism).
  - forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u): from LR (Arrow A B) t' we get LR B (app t' u); app t u --> app t' u (App1, lc u); IH(B) anti-reduction: LR B (app t u) ✓.
So anti-reduction doesn't need VR of values per se; it just propagates LR backward. And VR info flows forward via LR_step. OK.

But the earlier gap remains: how do we prove LR (Arrow A B) (abs ...) in the first place? Only from the T_Abs case of the fundamental theorem, where we know the body is semantically well-behaved. So T_Abs must prove the arrow clause for the abstraction: forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app (abs T1 body) u). Hmm, this is spiraling. Let me find a cleaner formulation.

**Cleaner idea**: Define LR so that the arrow clause is about *allations to LR arguments*, and prove a general lemma that LR is closed under the "left-spine" operations using well-founded induction on SN. Alternatively, use a *different but equivalent* definition that's easier to reason about:

**Definition via "hereditarily SN at type T" with values only:**

Classical CBV reducibility (e.g., from Pitts/Charguéraud):
```
V_T (values) and C_T (all terms) with C_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ (value t → V_T(t))? 
```
Hmm.

Alternatively, the "syntactic" approach with **neutral terms**: Standard Girard proof for call-by-name has LR(arrow) = forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t u), and the neutral case works because of the "Girard trick": neutral terms are in LR if their reducts are. For CBV, the neutral notion is: `neutral t := not value t`? Then "neutral terms are in LR iff their reducts are in LR" — for a neutral t at arrow type: LR(arrow) t iff SN t and forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u). For neutral t and any u ∈ LR A: app t u is neutral (app is never a value). Its reducts: t' u for t --> t' (App1), t u' for value t and u --> u' — but t is neutral → not value → so no App2; AppAbs needs t value ✗ (t is an app or if, wait t neutral just means not a value; AppAbs needs t = abs, and abs with lc is a value... if t = abs without lc then t is neutral and doesn't step; then app t u never steps... fine). So for neutral t: reducts of app t u ⊆ {app t' u | t --> t'}. Thus LR B (app t u) follows from "all reducts are in LR B" = a coinductive/accessible-style statement. This is exactly the "Girard's trick" with neutral terms, and it requires: LR T t ⟸ (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') when t is neutral... hmm, but for LR B (app t u) with app neutral, the reducts are app t' u, and we need those in LR B — which requires the arrow clause at t' — which is provided by the hypothesis "all reducts of t in LR (Arrow A B)" i.e., LR t' for t' reduct. So:

**Lemma (neutral closure)**: For any type T, if t is neutral (not a value... or maybe a stronger syntactic notion) and all one-step reducts of t are in LR T, and lc t, then LR T t.

Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: SN_intro ✓.
- Arrow A B: SN ✓; forall u ∈ LR A, LR B (app t u): prove by ... hmm, app t u is neutral, so by IH on B (structural!) we need: all reducts of app t u are in LR B. Reducts: app t' u for t --> t' (use arrow clause at LR t' ✓), or App2/AppAbs if t is a value — but t neutral, so no. Wait, careful: t neutral means not a value, but AppAbs requires t = abs AND value (lc) — if t = abs and not lc, then t is not a value but also app t u doesn't step via AppAbs because the premise lc fails. Great: "not a value" precisely rules out AppAbs as a *stepping* rule (since AppAbs's premises imply t is a value). ✓ So all reducts of app t u are app t' u.
  So by IH on B: LR B (app t u) provided (i) app t u is neutral ✓ (app is never a value), (ii) lc (app t u) ✓, (iii) all reducts of app t u ∈ LR B ✓ just shown.
  
  So `neutral closure` is provable by induction on the type, using well-foundedness only implicitly? Hmm, wait: I used IH on B to conclude LR B (app t u) from its reducts being in LR B. But the statement for B is "if all reducts of s are in LR B and s neutral and lc, then LR B s" — yes, that's the induction hypothesis at type B ✓. No well-founded induction needed! Nice. But hold on: "all reducts in LR B" for app t u: reducts are app t' u for t --> t'. We need LR B (app t' u), which comes from LR (Arrow A B) t' (the hypothesis at t) and LR A u ✓. 

So this "neutral closure" lemma is clean and powerful. It replaces the need for anti-reduction on arbitrary terms! Because in the T_Abs case:
- For non-value u: app (abs) u — hmm, but here the head is the abs, which IS a value. app (abs) u is neutral? app is never a value, so yes "app (abs) u" is neutral (as a term, it's not a value). Its reducts: App2 (if u --> u') and AppAbs (if u is a value). Hmm! AppAbs applies since the abs is a value (if lc) and u is a value. So the reducts include open body u. In the non-value-u case, AppAbs doesn't apply ✓, so reducts are app abs u' → by neutral closure: LR T2 (app abs u) from reducts in LR T2 — and those come from IH on SN u ✓. So we don't need to handle the arrow-clause nesting! Neutral closure handles it structurally.

- For value u: the reduct open body u is in LR T2 (from IH of fundamental theorem), but app abs u also has the reduct open body u, and app abs u is neutral. To apply neutral closure we need ALL reducts in LR. Reducts: AppAbs → open body u ✓; App2? u is a value, doesn't step ✗; App1? abs doesn't step ✗. So all reducts = {open body u} ∈ LR T2 ✓. Neutral closure gives LR T2 (app abs u). 

So the whole T_Abs case becomes: prove forall u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app abs u) by SN-induction on u; in the value case use the IH (semantic body fact) + neutral closure; in the non-value case use neutral closure + the SN-induction IH. Wait, but we need "all reducts" — for the non-value case, reducts of app abs u: App2 with u --> u' → LR T2 (app abs u') via SN-IH(u') and LR T1 u' via LR_step ✓. Good. But the SN-induction on u: the statement P u := LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app abs u); IH: forall u', u --> u' -> P u'. ✓.

Hmm, but actually, can we avoid the SN-induction on u? Use the neutral closure + SN induction: to prove LR T2 (app abs u) via neutral closure we need "all reducts in LR T2" — in the value case all reducts are just {open body u} ✓ (no induction needed!); in the non-value case, reducts are app abs u', needing P u' — well-founded on SN u ✓. So yes, SN-induction on u is needed for the non-value case. Fine.

Now let me double check the fundamental theorem statement and the T_Abs case in detail, since it's the crux.

**Fundamental theorem**: 
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_sat Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t).
```

Now, let me reconsider the definition of LR and whether the Fixpoint is accepted. `Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop := match T with ... forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t u)`. The recursive call `LR T1 u` — T1 is a subterm of T ✓; and `LR T2` ✓. This is fine with the guard checker.

Now, LR at Arrow includes `locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall u, ...)`. Since LR is a Fixpoint returning Prop, unfolding with `simpl` or `unfold LR` works. Alternatively, define LR as an Inductive with explicit constructors — might make destructing/inverting easier:
```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_Bool : forall t, locally_closed t -> SN t -> LR Ty_Bool t
| LR_Arrow : forall A B t, locally_closed t -> SN t -> (forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t u)) -> LR (Arrow A B) t.
```
Hmm, with an Inductive, proving LR_sn/LR_lc requires inversion, and the induction principle might be awkward (LR appears negatively in the Arrow constructor, so the induction principle would be weak, but we don't need induction on LR). Inversion on LR would work: `inversion HLR as [...]` gives the components. But inversion on an inductive with a negative occurrence is fine.

The Fixpoint version makes `destruct T` in goals easy and gives projections via `destruct HLR as [lc [sn clause]]`. I think the Fixpoint is fine. Let me use the Fixpoint.

Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall k j (t : tm), k <= j -> lc_at k t -> lc_at j t.

  - apply lc_bvar. lia.  (* i < k <= j *)
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; assumption.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IHlc_at_at. lia.  (* Hmm, the IH naming *)
  ...
Qed.

```
Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall k (t : tm), lc_at k t -> forall j, k <= j -> lc_at j t.
Proof. intros k t H. induction H; intros j Hle. ... Qed.
```

Lemma lc_open_close : forall t k x,
  lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  intros t. induction t; intros k x H.

    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. apply lc_bvar. lia.
    + inversion H. subst. apply lc_bvar. lia.   (* H : lc_at k (bvar n) → n < k *)
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - simpl in H. inversion H. subst. apply lc_app; [apply IHt1 | apply IHt2]; assumption.
  - simpl in H. inversion H. subst. apply lc_abs. apply IHt. assumption.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - simpl in H. inversion H; subst; apply lc_if; eauto.
Qed.
```

Now `subst`:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.
```

`fvars`:
```
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => (x :: nil)%list
  | tm_app t1 t2 => (fvars t1 ++ fvars t2)%list
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => (fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3)%list
  end.
```

```
Lemma SN_if : forall t1 t2 t3,
  strongly_normalizing t1 ->
  strongly_normalizing t2 ->
  strongly_normalizing t3 ->
  strongly_normalizing (tm_if t1 t2 t3).
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 t3 H1.
  induction H1 as [t1 Hred IH].
  intros H2 H3.
  apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst.
  inversion Hst as [T0 t v Hlc Hval | t1' t1'' t2' Hstep Hlc2 | v1 t2' t2'' Hval Hstep | t1' t2' Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1' t2' Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1' t1'' t2' t3' Hstep Hlc2 Hlc3]; subst.
  ...
```
Wait, the constructors of step:
```
| ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, locally_closed (tm_abs T t) -> value v -> tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open t v
| ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2
| ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> tm_app v1 t2 --> tm_app v1 t2'
| ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, locally_closed t1 -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1
| ST_IfFalse : forall t1 t2, locally_closed t1 -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_if tm_false t1 t2 --> t2
| ST_If : forall t1 t1' t2 t3, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> locally_closed t3 -> tm_if t1 t2 t3 --> tm_if t1' t2 t3
```
Inversion on `step (tm_if t1 t2 t3) t'`:
- ST_AppAbs: requires `tm_if t1 t2 t3 = tm_app (tm_abs T t) v` — contradiction (discriminate) — inversion should close it.
- ST_App1: `tm_if t1 t2 t3 = tm_app t1' t2'` — contradiction.
- ST_App2: contradiction.
- ST_IfTrue: `tm_if t1 t2 t3 = tm_if tm_true t1' t2'` → t1 = tm_true, t2 = t1', t3 = t2', t' = t1'. Goal: SN t1' = SN t2 ✓ H2.
- ST_IfFalse: similar ✓ H3.
- ST_If: t1 = t1, t2 = t2', t3 = t3'; premise t1 --> t1''; t' = tm_if t1'' t2 t3. Goal SN (if t1'' t2 t3): apply IH with Hred t1'' Hstep ✓... IH : forall t', t1 --> t' -> (forall t2 t3, SN t2 -> SN t3 -> SN (if t' t2 t3))?

Lemma LR_if : forall T t1 t2 t3,
  LR Ty_Bool t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> LR T (tm_if t1 t2 t3).
Proof.
  intros T t1 t2 t3 H1 H2 H3.
  assert (Hmain : forall (t1 : tm), strongly_normalizing t1 ->
     forall T t2 t3, LR Ty_Bool t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> LR T (tm_if t1 t2 t3)).
  { intros t1 Hsn. induction Hsn as [t1 Hred IH].
    intros T t2 t3 H1 H2 H3.
    cbn [LR]. (* unfolds LR at the head, but T is a variable — cbn might not unfold since T is not a constructor. Use destruct T. *)
    destruct T as [| A B].
    - (* Bool *) simpl. split.
      + simpl. apply lc_if; [apply (proj1 H1) | apply (proj1 H2) | apply (proj1 H3)].
        (* Hmm, LR Ty_Bool t1 = lc t1 ∧ SN t1; proj1 gives lc ✓; LR Ty_Bool t2 similarly *)
      + apply SN_if; [apply (proj2 H1) | apply (proj2 H2) | apply (proj2 H3)].
    - (* Arrow A B *) simpl. split; [ | split].
      + apply lc_if; [proj1 H1 | proj1 H2 | proj1 H3].
      + apply SN_if; [proj2 H1 | proj2 H2 | proj2 H3].
      + intros u HLRu.
        apply LR_neutral.
        * apply lc_app; [apply lc_if; [proj1 H1|proj1 H2|proj1 H3] | apply LR_lc; assumption].
        * intro Hv; inversion Hv.
        * intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep as [...]; subst.
          -- AppAbs: discriminate (source is app(abs), but our source is app (if ...) u).
          ...
  }
  apply Hmain; [apply LR_sn in H1; assumption | assumption | assumption | assumption].
```
Hmm, `inversion Hstep` might not magically handle discriminations; let's see. When inverting `step (tm_app (tm_if t1 t2 t3) u) t'`:
- ST_AppAbs: source `tm_app (tm_abs T0 body) v` unifies with `tm_app (tm_if t1 t2 t3) u` → gives `tm_if t1 t2 t3 = tm_abs T0 body` → inversion should detect the contradiction and close the goal automatically (because tm_if vs tm_abs are different constructors) ✓ — Coq's inversion does discriminate constructor clashes, yes.
- ST_App1: t1' := tm_if t1 t2 t3, t2' := u; premise: `tm_if t1 t2 t3 --> t1'0`; target: `tm_app t1'0 u`. Then I need to invert on the premise.
- ST_App2: `value v1` with `v1 = tm_if t1 t2 t3` → since value has no if constructor, inversion on the value hypothesis... Hmm, when Coq inverts the step, the equation `tm_app v1 t2 = tm_app (tm_if t1 t2 t3) u` gives v1 = tm_if ... and then the hypothesis `value v1` becomes `value (tm_if ...)` — Coq might not automatically derive a contradiction from `value (tm_if ...)` unless it's in the context and inversion is applied. Inversion of the step does substitute; the remaining goal with hypothesis `value (tm_if t1 t2 t3)` — not automatically contradictory. Coq's inversion does try to discharge by contradiction in some cases: `inversion Hstep` on an inductive whose constructor premises include `value v1` with v1 instantiated to a constructor-headed term of a different inductive type: inversion will detect that `value (tm_if ...)` has no constructors and hence is contradictory — I believe `inversion` does solve such subgoals automatically (it calls `discr` on impossible equalities of constructors, but `value (tm_if...)` isn't an equality of constructors; inversion would need to invert on the value hypothesis). Coq's `inversion` does automatically invert hypotheses when the constructor's arguments are impossible? Not sure. To be safe, I'll handle each branch explicitly with `exfalso` + inversion on the value hypothesis, or `match goal with ... end`.

Better approach: instead of `inversion Hstep as [...]` with all branches at once, I can do `inversion Hstep; subst; clear Hstep` and then handle remaining goals with `try (exfalso; inversion Hval)` etc. Hmm, but branch-specific handling is clearer with the `as` pattern. Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u t', tm_app t u --> t' ->
  (exists T body, t = tm_abs T body /\ t' = open body u) \/

  - (* App1 *) right; left. eauto.

Now the LR_step lemma:
```
Lemma LR_step : forall T t t', LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'.
Proof.
  intros T. induction T as [| A IHA B IHB]; intros t t' HLR Hstep.
  - simpl in HLR. simpl. split.
    + apply step_lc in Hstep; apply (proj1 Hstep).  (* or step_lc gives pair *)
    + apply SN_intro? no: SN t' from SN t: inversion on SN t? 
      destruct HLR as [Hlc Hsn]. inversion Hsn as [t Hred]. apply Hred; assumption.
      (* SN t = SN_intro (fun t' => ...); inversion gives the reduct function ✓ *)
  - simpl in HLR. destruct HLR as [Hlc [Hsn Hclause]]. simpl. split; [| split].
    + apply step_lc in Hstep; apply (proj2 Hstep).
    + inversion Hsn as [t0 Hred]. apply Hred. assumption.
    + intros u HLRu. 
      apply IHB with (t := tm_app t u).
      * apply Hclause; assumption.
      * apply ST_App1; [assumption | apply LR_lc; assumption].
Qed.
```
✓ Note ST_App1's premises: `t1 --> t1'` and `locally_closed t2` ✓.

Hmm, `inversion Hsn as [t0 Hred]` — Hsn : strongly_normalizing t; inversion gives Hred : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t' ✓. Actually `inversion Hsn` might be awkward; alternative: `induction Hsn`? Let's just do `inversion Hsn; subst; assumption`? Hmm. Cleaner: prove `SN_step : forall t t', SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'` := `fun t t' Hsn Hst => match Hsn with SN_intro _ Hred => Hred t' Hst end`. Let me define:

```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t t' Hsn Hst. destruct Hsn as [t0 Hred]. apply (Hred t' Hst). Qed.
```

Should be fine. Actually, `destruct` may fail to generalize... it should work since t is a variable and the index matches. Let's use `inversion Hsn as [t0 Hred]; subst` if needed.

```coq
(** ---------- Auxiliary definitions and lemmas (added) ---------- *)

(* Fresh atoms *)
Definition max_atom_list (L : list atom) : atom := fold_right max 0 L.

Lemma max_atom_list_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_atom_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - simpl in *. destruct Hin as [<- | Hin].
    + lia.
    + specialize (IH x Hin). lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_atom_list L)).
  intros Hin. apply max_atom_list_ge in Hin. lia.
Qed.
```

Definition max_atom_list (L : list atom) : atom := fold_right max 0 L.

Lemma open_rec_lc_at : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall j u, k <= j -> open_rec j u t = t.
```
Meaning: if all free bvars of t are < k ≤ j, then opening at index j does nothing. Prove by induction on the lc_at derivation (generalizing j, u). That's clean because induction on lc_at gives subterm lc premises as IHs.

Let's do:
```
Lemma open_rec_lc_at : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall j u, k <= j -> open_rec j u t = t.
Proof.
  intros k t H.
  induction H as [k' i Hlt | k' x | k' t1 t2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | k' T0 t1 H1 IH1 | k' | k' | k' t1 t2 t3 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 H3 IH3];
    intros j u Hle; simpl.
  - (* bvar i *) destruct (Nat.eqb j i) eqn:E. apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. lia. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2; auto.
  - rewrite IH1. reflexivity. lia.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2, IH3; auto.
Qed.
```
Then `open_rec_closed t Hlc k u : open_rec k u t = t` follows with k := 0 ✓ (0 <= j).

Wait, careful in the abs case: the goal is `open_rec j u (tm_abs T0 t1) = tm_abs T0 t1`, i.e., `tm_abs T0 (open_rec (S j) u t1) = tm_abs T0 t1` ← `open_rec (S j) u t1 = t1` from IH1 with `S k' <= S j` ✓ (from k' <= j).

Good.

Now open_rec_lc (opening preserves lc):
```
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall t k u, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
```
Prove by induction on t generalizing k, u. In the subterm cases we need to invert the lc_at hypotheses for subterms (for the app case: lc_at (S k) (app t1 t2) → both). Use inversion. Alternatively, prove by induction on the lc_at derivation with t generalized? Hmm, t is determined by the derivation. Let's do induction on t and invert.

Actually a cleaner way: prove by induction on `lc_at (S k) t`:
```
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall k t, lc_at (S k) t -> forall u, lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Proof.
  intros k t H.
  induction H as [...]; intros u Hu; simpl.
  - (* lc_bvar: i < S k *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst; assumption | apply lc_bvar; apply Nat.eqb_neq in E; lia].
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; auto.
  - (* lc_abs: premise lc_at (S (S k')) t1 *) apply lc_abs. apply IH... with k := S k'? 
```
Hmm, the IH in the abs case is `forall u, lc_at k' u -> lc_at k' (open_rec k' u t1)` (from the premise), but we need `lc_at (S k') (open_rec (S k') u t1)` — that's IH with k := S k', which isn't directly available. So induction on the derivation is awkward for the abs case; better to have a generalized statement with k universally quantified but the derivation at the *same* k... Hmm.

Standard approach: state it as `forall k t, lc_at (S k) t -> forall u, lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)` and do induction on t (structural), inverting the lc_at hypothesis to get subterm properties. The abs case: t = abs T0 t1, lc_at (S k) (abs T0 t1) → lc_at (S (S k)) t1, then IH t1 with k := S k and u := u: needs lc_at (S k) u (from lc_at k u by weaken) ✓, giving lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u t1) → lc_abs → lc_at k (abs T0 (open_rec (S k) u t1)) ✓.

So induction on t with inversion. For the app case: invert H to get lc_at (S k) t1 and lc_at (S k) t2 ✓.

Let me write it with `induction t; intros k u Ht Hu; simpl in *`.
Hmm, the statement `forall t k u, ...`: `intros t; induction t; intros k u Ht Hu` — but wait, for the induction to be on t with k, u generalized ✓.

In each case, Ht : lc_at (S k) <constructor>. `inversion Ht as [...]` or destruct. For bvar: Ht : lc_at (S k) (tm_bvar n) → n < S k (inversion gives Hlt). Then destruct (eqb k n).

OK.

```coq
(* ==================== Added lemmas ==================== *)

(** Fresh atoms *)

Definition max_atom_list (L : list atom) : atom := fold_right max 0 L.

Lemma max_atom_list_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_atom_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - destruct Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + lia.
    + apply IH in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (max_atom_list L)). intros Hin.
  apply max_atom_list_ge in Hin. lia.
Qed.

(** Local closure *)

Lemma lc_at_weaken : forall k (t : tm), lc_at k t -> forall j, k <= j -> lc_at j t.
Proof.
  intros k t H.
  induction H as [k' i Hlt | k' x | k' t1 t2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | k' T0 t1 H1 IH1 | k' | k' | k' t1 t2 t3 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 H3 IH3];
    intros j Hle.
  - apply lc_bvar; lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; lia.
  - apply lc_abs; apply IH1; lia.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - apply lc_if; [apply IH1 | apply IH2 | apply IH3]; lia.
Qed.

Lemma open_rec_lc_at : forall k (t : tm), lc_at k t -> forall j u, k <= j -> open_rec j u t = t.
Proof.
  intros k t H.
  induction H as [k' i Hlt | k' x | k' t1 t2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | k' T0 t1 H1 IH1 | k' | k' | k' t1 t2 t3 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 H3 IH3];

  - destruct (Nat.eqb j i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. lia.
    + reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2; auto.
  - erewrite IH1; [reflexivity | lia].
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2, IH3; auto.
Qed.

Lemma open_rec_closed : forall t, locally_closed t -> forall j u, open_rec j u t = t.
Proof.
  intros t H j u. apply open_rec_lc_at with (k := 0); auto. lia.
Qed.

Lemma lc_open_close : forall t k x,
  lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  intros t. induction t; intros k x H; simpl in *.

    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. apply lc_bvar. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion H as [k' i Hlt]. apply lc_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.

  - inversion H as [k' T0 body H1]. apply lc_abs. apply IHt. assumption.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.

Qed.

Lemma value_lc : forall v, value v -> locally_closed v.
Proof. intros v H. induction H as [T t Hlc | | ]. - assumption. - apply lc_true. - apply lc_false. Qed.

Lemma value_no_step : forall v t', value v -> ~ (v --> t').
Proof.
  intros v t' H. induction H as [T t Hlc | | ]; intro Hstep.

  - inversion Hstep.

Lemma value_SN : forall v, value v -> strongly_normalizing v.
Proof.
  intros v H. apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. exfalso. eapply value_no_step; eassumption.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma step_preserves_lc : forall t t', t --> t' -> locally_closed t /\ locally_closed t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H as [T t v Hlc Hval | t1 t1' t2 Hstep IH | v1 t2 t2' Hval Hstep IH | t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IH Hlc2 Hlc3].
  - (* ST_AppAbs *) simpl. split.
    + apply lc_app; [assumption | apply value_lc; assumption].
    + apply open_rec_lc.
      * inversion Hlc as [k T0 body H]. assumption.
      * apply value_lc; assumption.
  - (* ST_App1 *) destruct IH as [IH1 IH2]. simpl. split; apply lc_app; assumption.
  - (* ST_App2 *) destruct IH as [IH1 IH2]. simpl. split; apply lc_app; [apply value_lc; assumption | assumption].
     (* careful: goal lc (app v1 t2) and lc (app v1 t2') *)
  - (* ST_IfTrue *) simpl. split; [apply lc_if; [apply lc_true | assumption | assumption] | assumption].
  - (* ST_IfFalse *) simpl. split; [apply lc_if; [apply lc_false | assumption | assumption] | assumption].
  - (* ST_If *) destruct IH as [IH1 IH2]. simpl. split; apply lc_if; assumption.
Qed.
```
Check ST_App2: IH : locally_closed t2 /\ locally_closed t2' (from premise `t2 --> t2'`), Hval : value v1. Goal lc (tm_app v1 t2) ∧ lc (tm_app v1 t2'). ✓.

ST_If: IH : lc t1 ∧ lc t1' (from t1 --> t1'), Hlc2, Hlc3. Goal: lc (if t1 t2 t3) ∧ lc (if t1' t2 t3). lc_if needs lc t1, lc t2, lc t3 ✓ and lc t1' ✓.

Note: `induction H as [...]` for step: the constructors' args:
- ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, locally_closed (tm_abs T t) -> value v -> tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open t v → args: T, t, v, Hlc, Hval (no recursive premise → no IH) ✓
- ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> ... → args: t1, t1', t2, Hstep, IH, Hlc ✓
- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> ... → args: v1, t2, t2', Hval, Hstep, IH ✓
- ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, lc t1 -> lc t2 -> ... → t1, t2, Hlc1, Hlc2 ✓
- ST_IfFalse ✓
- ST_If : forall t1 t1' t2 t3, t1 --> t1' -> lc t2 -> lc t3 -> ... → t1, t1', t2, t3, Hstep, IH, Hlc2, Hlc3 ✓ (the IH comes right after the recursive premise).

OK.

Let me write step_app_inv and step_if_inv early.

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u t',
  tm_app t u --> t' ->
  (exists T0 body, t = tm_abs T0 body /\ locally_closed (tm_abs T0 body) /\ value u /\ t' = open body u)
  \/ (exists t1', t --> t1' /\ t' = tm_app t1' u)
  \/ (exists u', value t /\ u --> u' /\ t' = tm_app t u').
Proof.
  intros t u t' H. inversion H as [T0 body v Hlc Hval | t1 t1' t2 Hstep Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hval Hstep]; subst.
  - left. eauto.
  - right. left. eauto.
  - right. right. eauto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, in the inversion `as` pattern, the constructor args include the conclusion-related variables. Let's think: `inversion H` where H : tm_app t u --> t'. For ST_AppAbs: the conclusion is `tm_app (tm_abs T0 body) v --> open body v` unified with `tm_app t u --> t'`: gives t = tm_abs T0 body, u = v, t' = open body v ✓. The `as` pattern names T0 body v Hlc Hval. Then `subst` — hmm, `subst` would substitute t := tm_abs T0 body etc. Since t and u are intro'd variables, subst will rewrite them. Then `left; eauto` ✓.

Hmm, actually `inversion H as [...]` might also generate equalities. Let's just try; if it's problematic, I'll write `inversion H; subst` and then use `eauto`/explicit.

Alternatively, a simpler robust approach: `inversion H; subst; [left; eauto | right; left; eauto | right; right; eauto]`. But inversion may generate a different number of goals. We'll iterate.

Similarly step_if_inv:
```
Lemma step_if_inv : forall t1 t2 t3 t',
  tm_if t1 t2 t3 --> t' ->
  (t1 = tm_true /\ t' = t2) \/ (t1 = tm_false /\ t' = t3) \/ (exists t1', t1 --> t1' /\ t' = tm_if t1' t2 t3).
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 t3 t' H. inversion H; subst; eauto.

open_rec_lc:
```
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall t k u,
  lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Proof.
  intros t. induction t as [i | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T0 t1 IH1 | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3];
    intros k u Ht Hu; simpl in *.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. assumption.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. apply lc_bvar. apply lc_at_bvar_inv in Ht. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_at_app_inv in Ht. destruct Ht as [H1 H2]. apply lc_app.
    + apply IH1; assumption.
    + apply IH2; assumption.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IH1.
    + apply lc_at_abs_inv in Ht. assumption.
    + apply lc_at_weaken with (k := k); auto. lia.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - apply lc_at_if_inv in Ht. destruct Ht as [H1 [H2 H3]]. apply lc_if.
    + apply IH1; assumption.
    + apply IH2; assumption.
    + apply IH3; assumption.
Qed.
```
Careful: `lc_at_bvar_inv` requires `lc_at k' (tm_bvar i)`; here Ht : lc_at (S k) (tm_bvar i) → gives i < S k ✓; combined with i ≠ k → i < k ✓.

Lemma lc_open_close : forall t k x,
  lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  intros t. induction t as [...]; intros k x H; simpl in *.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. apply lc_bvar. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. apply lc_bvar. apply lc_at_bvar_inv in H. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_at_app_inv in H. destruct H as [H1 H2]. apply lc_app; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; assumption.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IH1. apply lc_at_abs_inv in H. assumption.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
  - apply lc_at_if_inv in H. destruct H as [H1 [H2 H3]]. apply lc_if; [apply IH1 | apply IH2 | apply IH3]; assumption.

Lemma subst_open_single : forall t sigma x u,
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->

```
Proof.
  intros t sigma x u Hsig Hu Hfresh.
  set (sigma' := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y).
  assert (Hsig' : forall y, locally_closed (sigma' y)).
  { intros y. unfold sigma'. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E; [assumption | apply Hsig]. }
  unfold open.
  rewrite (subst_open_rec t 0 (tm_fvar x) sigma' Hsig').
  assert (Hx : subst sigma' (tm_fvar x) = u).
  { unfold sigma'. simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. }
  rewrite Hx.
  rewrite (subst_fresh t sigma x u Hfresh).
  reflexivity.

```
Proof.
  intros t sigma x u Hsig Hu Hfresh.
  assert (Hsig' : forall y, locally_closed ((fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) y)).
  { intros y. simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E; [assumption | apply Hsig]. }
  unfold open.
  rewrite (subst_open_rec t 0 (tm_fvar x) (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) Hsig').
  assert (Hx : subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) (tm_fvar x) = u).
  { simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. }
  rewrite Hx.
  rewrite (subst_fresh t sigma x u Hfresh).
  reflexivity.
Qed.
```
